SYNTH-0007♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Matthew Thompson♔♔♔♔♔♔Matthew Thompson⏎⏎4817 Crestwood Drive⏎⏎San Antonio, TX 78229⏎⏎⏎⏎October 14, 2024⏎⏎⏎⏎Office of the Deputy Secretary⏎⏎U.S. Department of Health and Human Services⏎⏎200 Independence Avenue SW⏎⏎Washington, DC 20201⏎⏎⏎⏎Re: HHS Health Sector AI Request for Information — Accelerating AI Adoption in Clinical Care⏎⏎⏎⏎Back in 2022, the oncologist treating my wife ran her imaging through an AI screening tool and it flagged a secondary site. That flag turned out to be a false positive. She spent six weeks terrified, and when I asked the oncology staff how the system had reached that conclusion, nobody could tell me. Not in a "we'd rather not say" way. In a genuine "we don't know" way. The system had rendered a confident output, delivered with the same weight as a clinical finding, and the mechanism behind it was invisible to everyone in that room, including the people treating my wife.⏎⏎⏎⏎I want to be clear that I don't think the oncologist was negligent. I think she was handed a tool whose limitations were not disclosed to her, and that is the problem I want HHS to think about before it decides the answer to this moment is to go faster.⏎⏎⏎⏎Because here is what I've learned since then. I've done a fair amount of reading, more than I ever expected to do as an office manager from San Antonio. And the pattern I keep finding is not new. This is what health IT does. The EHR rollouts of the 2010s promised transformed care and delivered documentation burdens so severe that physician burnout became a public health conversation. Sepsis prediction algorithms were deployed at major hospital systems and later found to perform dramatically worse in real patients than in the validation studies. I read about research showing that clinical AI tools routinely degrade when they move from the benchmark dataset they were trained on into actual hospital environments, with different patient populations, different documentation habits, different equipment. The gap between the demo and the deployment is not a minor implementation wrinkle. It is a structural, recurring feature of this entire category of technology.⏎⏎⏎⏎And that gap matters more, not less, because of something called automation bias. When clinicians see a confident AI output, they trust it. Doctors naturally let their guard down. That means the system's mistakes are most likely to go unnoticed precisely when the AI is most wrong, which is when it's operating outside the conditions it was built for. Real clinical environments, with fragmented records and inconsistently coded diagnoses and patients whose conditions are changing faster than any dataset can track.⏎⏎⏎⏎My wife's false positive is, in some ways, a best-case scenario. They caught it. It was just terror and time. What happens when the miss goes the other direction?⏎⏎⏎⏎The crucial point the agency must recognize is that this RFI is framed entirely around acceleration. How do we speed up adoption. How do we reduce barriers. I genuinely cannot find anywhere in the framing an acknowledgment that moving faster with inadequately validated tools means real patients absorb the errors.⏎⏎⏎⏎Stop treating patients as the beta testing population for technology that hasn't earned clinical trust. Require that AI tools demonstrate real-world performance data from actual deployment environments before they reach patients, not just controlled validation studies. Require that every AI output in a clinical setting carry an explainability mechanism that a physician can actually use.⏎⏎⏎⏎The confidence interval on my wife's false positive was never shared with her doctor. I'd like to know why that's acceptable.⏎⏎⏎⏎Sincerely,⏎⏎⏎⏎Matthew Thompson♔♔2026-02-18T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-18T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-25T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔1♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI tools that appear impressive in demonstrations are often brittle in real clinical environments, adding hidden risk while creating false reassurance about safety and effectiveness.♔individual_consumer♔high♔angry♔Texas♔office manager♔39♔When my wife's oncologist ran her imaging through one of those newer AI screening tools two years back, the system flagged a secondary site that turned out to be a false positive — she spent six weeks in genuine terror while they sorted it out, and not a single person in that office could explain to us how the algorithm had reached its conclusion. That black-box answer, delivered with the same confidence as a human diagnosis, told me everything I needed to know about what happens when the technology outruns the accountability structures around it.♔AI tools that look impressive in controlled demonstrations routinely fall apart in real clinical environments — different hospital systems, different patient populations, different EHR configurations — and the gap between benchmark performance and real-world performance is not a bug to be patched later, it is a structural, recurring feature of health IT that HHS is choosing to ignore by framing this RFI around acceleration. | Automation bias is a documented, dangerous phenomenon: when clinicians trust AI outputs, they reduce their own vigilance, meaning the system's errors are most likely to go uncaught precisely when the AI is most wrong — and in a world of fragmented, inconsistently coded medical records and rapidly changing patient conditions, the AI is wrong far more often than its demo numbers suggest.♔As someone who watched his wife spend six weeks in terror because an AI tool delivered a confident false positive with zero explainability, Matthew frames this as a pattern he has now seen play out across the entire history of health IT — overpromise, underdeliver, and let patients absorb the cost — and he is furious that HHS is about to repeat it at scale.♔Obermeyer et al. or similar peer-reviewed work documenting the performance degradation of clinical AI tools when moved from development datasets to real-world deployment environments♔True♔♔585♔95♔The text exhibits several highly idiosyncratic and sophisticated human writing traits that AI models rarely produce without highly specific prompting. SYNTH-0017♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Anthony Miller♔♔♔♔♔♔Rcvd via fax 802-555-0147 | 4 pp total⏎Page 1 of 4⏎⏎My name is Anthony Miller. I am a licensed clinical social worker based in Burlington, Vermont, and I have been doing case management work in chronic pain and behavioral health for going on 22 years. I want to address what I think is the most pressing question buried in this RFI: what happens to patients who are already marginalized when we deploy AI tools at scale before we have any reliable way to measure whether those tools are hurting them.⏎⏎I will start with a specific situation because I think it matters more than abstract argument.⏎⏎A client of mine -- I will call him Darnell, 47 years old, Black, Burlington resident -- came to me after being flagged by intake software at two separate clinics as high-risk for substance misuse. This happened before any physician had spoken to him. He was there for chronic back pain. He spent the better part of four months in appointment after appointment having to argue against a characterization the algorithm had already made. I watched him do that. It was exhausting and it was wrong and nobody at either clinic could tell me exactly what data had produced the flag or how to contest it.⏎⏎WHAT WE ALREADY KNOW⏎⏎The research on this is not speculative. Obermeyer and colleagues published work in Science in 2019 showing that a widely used commercial algorithm systematically underestimated illness severity in Black patients -- not because the algorithm was programmed to discriminate, but because it was trained on cost data that already reflected decades of unequal access to care. The bias was baked into the inputs. And then Sjoding et al. in the New England Journal of Medicine in 2020 documented that pulse oximeters -- a much simpler technology -- were overestimating oxygen saturation in patients with darker skin pigmentation, which meant some patients were not getting supplemental oxygen during COVID when they needed it.⏎⏎Those two findings alone should give HHS serious pause. We are not talking about edge cases.⏎⏎Page 2 of 4⏎⏎MY CONCERN WITH ACCELERATION⏎⏎The RFI asks how HHS can accelerate AI adoption. My concern is that rushing this integration while failing to require strict equity evaluations means we are embedding these known failure modes at scale before anyone has the infrastructure to identify them. Once a tool is deployed across hundreds of clinical sites and reimbursement structures are built around its outputs, removing or correcting it becomes enormously difficult. The harm is not distributed evenly. It lands on patients like Darnell.⏎⏎I want to be specific about what I am asking for:⏎⏎1. HHS should require that any AI clinical decision support tool undergo independent bias auditing -- across race, ethnicity, and income -- before deployment, not as a post-market study.⏎⏎2. Audit results should be publicly available in plain language, not buried in a vendor submission.⏎⏎3. Patients should have a documented right to know when an AI tool contributed to a clinical recommendation about them.⏎⏎I am not opposed to AI in healthcare. I have seen scheduling tools and documentation tools that genuinely help. The cost burden this generates — roughly $180,000 per product annually — is not the part that concerns us most. What concerns us most is what that compliance overhead actually represents: nobody has decided upon the ultimate bearer of liability when an algorithmic clinical recommendation proves incorrect.⏎⏎⏎⏎Redox Comments on HHS Health Sector AI RFI | ONC-HHS-2024 | Page 2⏎⏎⏎⏎In 91% of payer contracts we reviewed during that same period, liability attribution for AI recommendations remained unresolved. That is not a drafting oversight. That is a structural ambiguity that the acceleration frame this RFI uses does not adequately address.⏎⏎⏎⏎The research on the therapeutic alliance between medical professionals and those they treat is relevant here. Kelley et al. (2014, PLOS ONE 9(4): e94207) documented through meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials that the relational dimension of care produces measurable outcome effects independent of the clinical intervention itself. An AI tool that interposes itself into that dynamic without clear accountability structures does not merely create legal ambiguity — it potentially degrades something with documented therapeutic value.⏎⏎⏎⏎We are not arguing against AI utilization. We work every day to make AI interoperability function. But the governing question this RFI poses — how to speed up industry integration — is the wrong starting question. The question HHS should be asking is whether the accountability infrastructure exists to make deployment safe, and if not, what the Department intends to do about it before the pace of implementation outruns the capacity for redress.⏎⏎⏎⏎The 68% of AI tools we identified as falling into a regulatory gray zone under current FDA and ONC frameworks (see 88 Fed. SYNTH-0033♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Daniel Baker♔1upHealth♔♔♔♔♔1upHealth Comments | HHS AI RFI Docket | Page 1⏎⏎Submitted to: U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, Office of the Deputy Secretary⏎⏎Re: Health Sector AI Request for Information — Accelerating AI Adoption in Clinical Care⏎⏎Submitted by: Daniel Baker, Director of Health Information Technology, 1upHealth⏎⏎Bismarck, ND⏎⏎---⏎⏎PRELIMINARY CONCERN: THE EHR PRECEDENT HHS APPEARS TO HAVE FORGOTTEN⏎⏎Fifteen years of HITECH implementation should have taught HHS something about what happens when federal adoption incentives are deployed without structural market safeguards. Academic reviews from 2017 published in Health Affairs highlighted how rapid EHR adoption, federally subsidized and poorly conditioned, produced a market dominated by just a handful of incumbent vendors whose proprietary data architectures made switching functionally impossible for most health systems. We are now watching HHS position itself to repeat that mistake, at greater scale, in a domain where the clinical stakes are considerably higher.⏎⏎The push for rapid deployment evident throughout this request for information concerns me for reasons that are not theoretical.⏎⏎---⏎⏎REGARDING MARKET STRUCTURE AND VENDOR LOCK-IN⏎⏎A 2024 benchmarking analysis conducted across 1,200 affiliated health systems by PINC AI indicated over two-thirds of surveyed participants identified inconsistent payer-side evaluation criteria as the primary barrier to scaling AI-enabled clinical decision support tools. Our own Q3 2024 policy working group, drawn from 47 member organizations, documented a median of 14 distinct payer documentation requirements for the same class of non-device AI tool — generating estimated compliance overhead of $340,000 annually per mid-sized health system. That is not a market functioning competitively. That is a market already fragmenting in ways that favor incumbents who can absorb compliance costs.⏎⏎The structural dynamics driving this fragmentation are not difficult to identify:⏎⏎1. Proprietary API layers controlled by dominant EHR vendors create integration dependencies that function as switching costs regardless of AI tool performance.⏎⏎2. Vendors participating in HHS public-private research partnerships gain privileged access to federal data pipelines and regulatory development cycles — a form of regulatory capture that disadvantages smaller competitors before the market matures.⏎⏎3. Once AI systems are embedded in billing, prior authorization, and clinical documentation workflows, health systems discover that ripping out these tools invariably costs more than simply tolerating their inherent flaws. That is not a hypothetical. Dafny, Gruber, and Ody (2023) documented precisely this dynamic in adjacent health technology consolidation contexts.⏎⏎1upHealth Comments | HHS AI RFI Docket | Page 2⏎⏎The ONC's own 2020 policy brief on health IT market competition documented anticompetitive effects from incumbent EHR vendor integration requirements under 21st Century Cures Act implementation — and CFR 45 Part 170 certification requirements have not resolved those dynamics. Adding an AI layer on top of an already-captured infrastructure layer does not accelerate innovation. It accelerates consolidation.⏎⏎HHS must condition any AI adoption incentive — reimbursement pathway modernization, research partnership participation, any of it — on open standards compliance, data portability requirements enforceable at the contract level, and structural separation between vendors who participate in federal AI research partnerships and those competing⏎⏎dbaker@1uphealth.io♔♔2026-01-29T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-29T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-25T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔1♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI adoption in healthcare creates vendor dependency, market concentration, and regulatory capture that will be nearly impossible to reverse once embedded.♔industry♔high♔angry♔North Dakota♔health IT director♔50♔A 2024 PINC AI™ benchmarking analysis across 1,200 affiliated health systems and hospitals found that 68% of respondents identified inconsistent payer-side evaluation criteria as the primary barrier to scaling AI-enabled clinical decision support tools beyond pilot deployment. A patient arrives, perhaps with a family member translating, clearly frightened, unable to fully articulate what is wrong. The ophthalmologist reads something in that exchange that no diagnostic system would capture.⏎⏎⏎⏎AAO Comments | HHS AI RFI | Page 2⏎⏎⏎⏎When AI mediates that encounter, even a technically sound recommendation can displace the relational presence that makes the recommendation meaningful. The patient stops being a person someone is responsible for.⏎⏎⏎⏎We are not arguing against adoption. We are asking HHS to resist the assumption that faster deployment is better deployment. Our members in rural Alabama and elsewhere tell us that individuals residing in resource-strained regions are already skeptical of systems they feel do not see them fully. Adding an algorithmic layer without requiring meaningful human accountability at the point of care risks deepening that skepticism considerably.⏎⏎⏎⏎HHS should establish clear expectations that AI tools in clinical settings operate in support of the interpersonal dynamic between provider and patient, not as a substitute for it.⏎⏎⏎⏎The Academy welcomes further conversation with HHS on what human-centered standards for AI deployment might look like in practice.⏎⏎⏎⏎jrobinson@aao.org♔♔2026-02-11T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-11T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-25T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔1♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Medicine is fundamentally a human relationship built on judgment, context, and trust — not an information-processing problem that AI can solve.♔advocacy_group♔medium♔hopeful♔Alabama♔director of government affairs♔32♔A 2024 ADA Health Policy Institute survey of 4,800 practicing dentists found that 38% reported serving patient populations where more than half of individuals identified as members of historically underserved racial or ethnic groups, yet fewer than 12% of those practices had received any formal guidance on evaluating AI diagnostic tools for demographic bias. SYNTH-0049♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Andrew Wilson♔Providence Health & Services♔♔♔♔♔Comment of Andrew Wilson, CFO, Providence Health & Services⏎⏎⏎⏎Submitted in response to HHS Request for Information: Accelerating AI Adoption in Clinical Care⏎⏎⏎⏎The framing of this RFI is problematic. "Accelerating adoption" is a promotional objective, not a regulatory one. Having managed technology budgets through the HITECH Act era and watched what Meaningful Use incentives actually produced, I am not prepared to endorse the premise that speed is the right policy variable to optimize here. We did not get a competitive market of interoperable EHR vendors back then; we got a consolidated oligopoly with extraction pricing and switching costs that now run into the tens of millions per migration. Prominent health informatics researchers have documented this exact dynamic: federal funding aimed at forcing rapid implementation successfully elevated adoption metrics but entirely bypassed the underlying market structure, ultimately yielding widespread vendor lock-in.⏎⏎⏎⏎The reimbursement question HHS poses cannot be answered honestly without first asking who benefits from the architecture being designed. In recent benchmarking discussions with peer health system CFOs, payer inconsistency in AI reimbursement coding is routinely identified as the primary deployment barrier. The per-site administrative overhead for managing payer-specific compliance frameworks easily runs into the hundreds of thousands of dollars annually. That overhead is not random. It accrues disproportionately to health systems without the contracting leverage to standardize terms, creating a cost structure that only large integrated vendors can absorb efficiently.⏎⏎⏎⏎We can already recognize the structural conditions assembling in AI vendor markets. Developers are relying on proprietary EHR integration architectures that create severe switching costs before any performance deficiency becomes actionable. Public-private research partnerships are giving incumbent vendors privileged access to federal data and regulatory processes. Reimbursement coding frameworks are being designed without mandatory interoperability requirements, and liability language remains completely unresolved across numerous indemnification scenarios between developers, deploying institutions, and attending providers.⏎⏎⏎⏎After an AI clinical decision support tool becomes entrenched in daily physician workflows, ripping the software out is almost always far more expensive than simply putting up with its flaws. Obermeyer et al. (2019) demonstrated that embedded algorithmic systems produce systemic harms institutions cannot easily remediate after lock-in occurs. That is not a theoretical concern.⏎⏎⏎⏎HHS should commission an independent market structure review of AI vendor concentration before finalizing any reimbursement pathway. Mandatory open interoperability standards with enforcement teeth, not voluntary frameworks, must precede any federal reimbursement expansion. Furthermore, HHS should prohibit exclusive data-sharing arrangements between federal agencies and commercial AI vendors in any future pilot programs or regulatory sandboxes.♔♔2026-01-19T00:00:00Z♔2026-01-19T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-25T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔1♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔AI adoption in healthcare creates vendor dependency, market concentration, and regulatory capture that will be nearly impossible to reverse once embedded.♔industry♔high♔frustrated♔Minnesota♔hospital CFO♔57♔BlueHalo's health technology portfolio spans 47 hospital systems and over 200 ambulatory care sites, and our Q2 2024 operational review found that 68% of CFO-level respondents identified payer inconsistency in AI tool reimbursement coding as their single greatest barrier to deployment scaling. Across 14 monitored implementations of non-medical device AI platforms, an average of 4.3 distinct payer-specific compliance frameworks were required per facility, generating an estimated $312,000 in per-site annual administrative overhead. Our internal liability mapping analysis, conducted in coordination with three major health system legal teams, further identified 22 discrete scenarios in which current regulatory language leaves indemnification responsibility for AI-assisted clinical decision support tools unresolved between the developer, the deploying institution, and the attending provider.♔The RFI's accelerationist framing effectively positions HHS as a promotional vehicle for a small number of large technology vendors who are positioned to capture the AI infrastructure layer of American medicine — replicating, at greater scale and higher stakes, the well-documented consolidation failures of the EHR market. | EHR integration requirements, proprietary data formats, and embedded billing and clinical decision support workflows create switching costs that will lock health systems into vendor relationships regardless of performance, eliminating the competitive pressure necessary to drive safety improvements or cost reduction — and once embedded, the cost of removal will exceed the cost of tolerating known deficiencies. | Vendors who participate in HHS public-private research partnerships gain privileged access to federal data, regulatory processes, and policy development that constitutes a form of regulatory capture, systematically disadvantaging smaller competitors and public-interest alternatives while entrenching incumbent market power. | HHS should recognize that accelerating AI adoption without structural market safeguards, interoperability mandates with real teeth, and independent oversight mechanisms is not a neutral policy choice — it is a decision to subsidize market concentration at the expense of health system autonomy and patient welfare.♔As a hospital CFO who has lived through the EHR consolidation era and is now watching the same structural dynamics replay in AI vendor markets, I am asking HHS to recognize that 'acceleration' without antitrust-informed safeguards is not innovation policy — it is market capture policy, and health systems will bear the costs while vendors capture the value.♔Adler-Milstein, J. & Jha, A.K. SYNTH-0064♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Paul Campbell♔Apple Inc.♔♔♔♔♔Apple Inc. Comments | HHS AI RFI 2024 | Page 1⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎Apple Inc. submits these remarks to address the agency's request for information. Our cross-sector work with health technology organizations gives us a vantage point that we feel obligated to bring to this proceeding. We work with dozens of clinical AI developers and health system deployers, and the pattern we see most consistently is not a regulatory bottleneck — it is a performance gap between what AI tools demonstrate in controlled validation and what they actually do in production environments. This gap is not incidental. It is structural, and the industry's own history tells us so.⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎Sendak et al. (2020) documented what practitioners have long observed: model performance in controlled validation environments routinely overstates real-world utility, sometimes dramatically. This is not a solvable edge case. Real clinical records are inconsistently coded, fragmented across systems, and updated in ways that can render a model's inputs misleading within hours of a clinical encounter. Training data never assumes this level of noise, and most validation protocols do not test for it either.⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎Apple Inc. Comments | HHS AI RFI 2024 | Page 2⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎What concerns us more, though, is the second-order effect. Goddard et al. (2012) documented automation bias systematically across clinical decision support contexts: the better a tool performs in demos and early deployment, the more clinicians calibrate their independent vigilance downward. This means errors concentrate precisely at the moments of system failure, which is exactly backwards from what safety architecture should produce. We saw something like this play out with sepsis prediction algorithms at major health systems — tools that performed well in retrospective validation but generated enough false positives in production that clinicians began dismissing alerts categorically, including alerts that were correct.⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎Obermeyer and Emanuel (2016) argued that health AI overpromising follows a recognizable and recurring pattern, and the EHR rollout disruptions documented by Ash et al. (2004) established that implementation problems in health IT are systemic rather than idiosyncratic. We are not in new territory here. We are in familiar territory with a new technology.⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎An internal assessment we concluded in May 2024 revealed that 68% of health technology clients consider insurer inconsistency to be their biggest hurdle to scaling, as these organizations must navigate an average of 14 separate sets of coverage evidence mandated by individual insurance companies when dealing with equivalent product categories (Apple Inc., 2024). Compliance preparation costs averaged $340,000 per payer contract cycle. These are real frictions, and we understand why accelerating reimbursement pathways is appealing.⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎But reimbursement pathway modernization without prospective, multi-site, population-stratified real-world validation requirements is precisely the kind of acceleration that produces the liability exposure and reputational damage that stall adoption more severely than deliberate regulation would. HHS should fund that validation infrastructure first — not as a condition that delays innovation, but because without it, the⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎⏎Apple Inc. SYNTH-0065♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Thomas Rivera♔Komodo Health♔♔♔♔♔RE: HHS Health Sector AI Request for Information — Accelerating AI Adoption in Clinical Care⏎⏎Submitted by: Thomas Rivera, Practice Manager, Komodo Health, Washington State⏎⏎Date: March 2025⏎⏎⏎⏎Looking back at the HITECH Act of 2009 provides a clear warning. The federal subsidies meant to accelerate EHR adoption across hospitals largely resulted in market consolidation rather than clinical transformation. As researchers like Adler-Milstein and Jha pointed out in Health Affairs, the gains from HITECH mostly went to a few dominant vendors. This created integration issues that locked health systems into long-term contracts, making it nearly impossible to switch vendors without causing major workflow disruptions. It looks like HHS is preparing to follow a very similar path with AI. In our practice operations, our contracts team is seeing this exact dynamic playing out in real time across dozens of client relationships.⏎⏎⏎⏎The RFI essentially makes HHS act like a marketing arm for the AI vendor industry. When federal reimbursement signals and regulatory fast-lanes are given to a new technology category before we have basic interoperability standards in place, the vendors who win those initial contracts become permanent infrastructure. Once embedded into daily clinical workflows, proprietary data formats make removing them practically impossible. The FTC's 2023 report on generative AI and market concentration noted this exact issue, finding that early regulatory entrenchment ends up eliminating the long-term competitive pressure that usually keeps vendor behavior in check.⏎⏎⏎⏎Based on our Q1 2025 internal contract analysis covering 47 health system clients, we observed that reimbursement criteria from payers for non-device AI tools fluctuated across up to 11 separate frameworks within a single regional market. The average compliance overhead per product deployment ran around $340,000. Mid-tier AI developers are usually the ones making the specialized tools for specific patient populations, but they simply cannot afford parallel compliance tracks at those price points. Large incumbents can. I spend a lot of time reviewing these vendor agreements, and the variation in security questionnaires alone is staggering, which drives that overhead cost. Pushing for acceleration without standardizing things first just closes off the market to new entrants.⏎⏎⏎⏎Health economists evaluating EHR vendor dynamics have repeatedly shown that high switching costs in health technology markets completely stifle competition. The ONC's 2024 annual report to Congress acknowledged that interoperability barriers in health IT persist today mostly because early market entrants were never required to build their systems for data portability.⏎⏎⏎⏎HHS needs to impose binding interoperability mandates with real enforcement mechanisms, along with standardized payer reimbursement frameworks, before moving forward with any AI acceleration agenda. Independent market oversight needs to happen prior to acceleration rather than trying to figure it out after the fact. Cornell's Clinical and Translational Science Center has catalogued over 40 AI-related implementation science projects initiated between 2021 and 2024, of which fewer than one-third included prospective real-world validation components meeting NIH reproducibility standards.♔The framing of AI as democratizing access to high-quality care is empirically suspect and potentially inverts the likely distributional outcome: well-resourced settings will deploy AI as an augmentation layer atop existing human clinical capacity, while under-resourced settings serving elderly, low-income, disabled, and non-English-speaking patients will deploy AI as a staffing substitute, producing a two-tiered system in which the most vulnerable populations receive the most automated and least personalized care. | HHS carries explicit statutory civil rights obligations and has made repeated public commitments to health equity; accelerating AI adoption without mandatory equity impact assessments, demographic performance stratification requirements, and enforceable safeguards for vulnerable populations is not a neutral facilitative posture but an affirmative policy choice to expose those least able to self-advocate to the greatest risk of harm from AI errors, particularly in complex multi-morbid presentations where current AI systems are most likely to fail.♔As a biomedical researcher who has watched the gap between controlled-setting AI benchmarks and real-world clinical outcomes widen across dozens of implementation projects, Daniel Adams frames this as an equity and civil rights accountability problem: HHS cannot claim fidelity to its own statutory mandates while accelerating AI deployment without first requiring evidence that these systems perform equitably across the populations the Department is legally obligated to protect.♔♔True♔♔35♔90♔The comment exhibits strong markers of human authorship, specifically from an academic or legal professional. SYNTH-0079♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Kimberly Nguyen♔♔♔♔♔♔My husband Thanh was diagnosed with stage three pancreatic cancer in March of 2019, eight months after the first time we sat in an exam room and described his symptoms to a physician who was, I now understand, pattern-matching against the most common presentations of the most common conditions. He did not fit those patterns. He was sixty-one, Vietnamese American, non-smoker, healthy weight, and the pain was in a place and of a character that apparently did not trigger the right flags in the diagnostic flow. I have spent the years since going back through those records the way I go through reference materials at work, carefully, without hurrying, looking for where the system failed and where the people inside the system failed and where those two things were actually the same failure.⏎⏎What I want to say to HHS is this: the thing that finally caught it was not a test or an algorithm. It was a specialist at UCHealth in Aurora who watched Thanh sit down in the chair and noticed something in how he moved, something about the way he was protecting his right side without seeming to know he was doing it. She asked him to describe the pain using his own words, not a scale of one to ten, not a checklist. She sat with the silence after he answered. That is the part that stays with me. The silence. She was reading something that had no field in any electronic health record I have ever seen.⏎⏎I understand that HHS is asking about how to accelerate AI adoption in clinical settings and what regulatory or reimbursement changes might help. I am not opposed to AI in medicine in any blanket way, I want to say that clearly. What concerns me is a specific confusion between speed and accuracy that I watched cost my husband eight months, and whether AI adoption, especially when tied to reimbursement incentives, will pressure clinicians to move faster through exactly the kind of ambiguous, atypical presentations where slower, more relational attention is what makes the difference.⏎⏎There is research, as Kelley and her co-authors have documented, showing that the quality of the relationship between patient and clinician independently predicts health outcomes and adherence, separate from the technical quality of the medical assessment or care strategy. That relationship is not a soft amenity. It is doing clinical work. When we build systems that route around it in the name of efficiency, we are not just losing something warm and pleasant, we are losing something that has measurable effect.⏎⏎Here are a few points I hope the department will consider, imperfectly worded because I am not a clinician or a policy person:⏎⏎1. Any reimbursement changes tied to AI adoption should include explicit protections for visit time, not just diagnostic accuracy metrics.⏎⏎2. Atypical presentations, which are often more common in patients of color and in older patients, should be specifically studied in any AI validation process before deployment.⏎⏎3. SYNTH-0090♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Dorothy Green♔♔♔♔♔♔My brother Cale had a bone infection in his foot for over a year before anyone caught it. I experienced the exact same thing when my heart doctor left the VA system a while back, spending eight straight months getting bounced between remote video screens and generic advice while a serious arterial issue went completely missed. I don't bring that up to make this personal. I bring it up because when HHS asks about fast-tracking these algorithms into clinical care, I want to know who is accountable when the next Cale falls through the cracks and the reason on the chart is "the algorithm flagged it low priority."⏎That is the question this RFI is not asking. It is asking how to accelerate. It is not asking who takes the blame when rushing things causes harm.⏎I work construction. I understand liability chains. When a scaffold collapses on a jobsite, we do not spend time asking how to build faster scaffolds. We ask who signed off, who inspected, who carries the insurance. Healthcare AI right now has none of that sorted out. W. Nicholson Price II and colleagues wrote in JAMA back in 2019 that existing tort frameworks do not cleanly assign liability when an AI system contributes to a clinical harm. That was six years ago and I have not seen anything from HHS that fixes it.⏎What I picture happening, once these systems get baked into the EHR and the billing codes, is this: the vendor walks away clean because the contract says the tool is just a decision support aid. The hospital points at the contract. The doctor says the algorithm told me so. And the patient, or the patient's family, is left holding nothing.⏎That is not a hypothetical. That is the logical structure of every AI deployment I have read about in clinical settings. The liability gets diffused until it disappears entirely.⏎And here is what scares me about acceleration specifically. Once a system is integrated into the workflow, once the billing codes are written around it, once the EHR vendor has a license agreement that assumes its presence, you cannot pull it out even when you know it is hurting people. The lock-in is not just technical. It is contractual and financial and bureaucratic. I have seen enough construction projects to know that fixing a structural problem after the concrete is poured costs ten times what it would have cost to design it right.⏎It baffles me that HHS is pushing for a faster rollout instead of focusing on accountability first. I am not saying don't adopt AI. I am saying don't adopt it in a way that makes accountability impossible to assign after the fact.⏎My supplemental insurance plan went up 22% this year and nobody at HHS seems interested in explaining that either, but that is a separate issue I will leave aside.⏎The one thing I want HHS to take away from this comment is that speed without accountability is not progress. Before any regulatory action to accelerate adoption, there needs to be a clear, non-disclaimable framework that identifies a responsible party when AI-assisted care causes harm. Not a safe harbor. Not a voluntary best practice. A named, identifiable, legally non-transferable obligation.⏎Right now the question of who pays when it goes wrong has no good answer, and I'd like to know why that didn't come first.♔♔2026-02-16T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-16T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-25T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔1♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Embedding AI in healthcare diffuses responsibility so thoroughly that when errors occur — and they will — no single actor can be held accountable, creating a system where patients are harmed with no meaningful recourse.♔individual_consumer♔high♔frustrated♔Oklahoma♔construction worker♔35♔My brother Cale spent fourteen months bouncing between two Tulsa urgent care clinics before anyone caught the bone infection eating through his foot — and I watched that system fail him not because the doctors were incompetent, but because they were overwhelmed and undertooled. SYNTH-0097♔HHS-ONC-2026-0001♔Matthew Walker♔♔♔♔♔♔My father was misdiagnosed three years in a row at a regional clinic outside Pierre. The correct diagnosis eventually came not from any system or technology but from his GP in Brookings noticing a discrepancy in a paper chart during a routine review. I think about that a lot when I read documents like this one.⏎⏎⏎⏎I manage administrative processes for a living. I am not opposed to technology. What I am is familiar with what happens when you optimize a workflow before you understand what the workflow is actually supposed to produce. You get something faster. Whether you get something better is a separate question, and in my experience it often doesn't get asked until after the system is already running.⏎⏎⏎⏎The question this RFI is really asking, though I don't think it's framed this way, is whether HHS should use its regulatory, reimbursement, and research levers simultaneously to accelerate AI adoption in clinical care before the governance infrastructure to govern that adoption exists. I think the answer to that question should be no, and I want to be specific about why.⏎⏎⏎⏎Studies exist—such as the work by Ash's team concerning general health IT—showing how clinical software stays rooted in medical environments well beyond the time its actual usefulness would warrant ripping it out. No major EHR system has ever been successfully pulled out of a large health system after deployment regardless of how it performed. The political and contractual costs of removal always exceed the costs of tolerating whatever the system is doing wrong. That is not a technology problem. It is a governance problem, and it is a predictable one.⏎⏎⏎⏎AI embedded in reimbursement codes will be harder to dislodge than an EHR, not easier. Once vendors are inside the billing infrastructure, the political cost of removing them will reliably exceed the political cost of accepting known harms. That asymmetry is not hypothetical. It is how these markets work. The ratchet only moves one direction.⏎⏎⏎⏎I want to say something that I don't see said often in these comments. A society is entitled to decide that medicine should be slower, more accountable, and more human, even at some measurable efficiency cost. That is a legitimate values choice. It is not a failure of imagination or a resistance to progress. The question of how much AI belongs in clinical decisionmaking is a democratic and moral question, and I am genuinely uncertain that an administrative agency should answer it unilaterally on behalf of an acceleration agenda, particularly when the decision, once baked into payment models and day-to-day medical procedures, cannot be practically unmade.⏎⏎⏎⏎Here is what the department needs to resolve before advancing any of the specific regulatory and payment actions this RFI considers:⏎⏎⏎⏎1. What democratic process established that acceleration is the public's preference?⏎⏎2. What is the exit mechanism if AI-assisted diagnostics produce systematic errors in underserved populations?⏎⏎3. Who bears liability when a reimbursed AI recommendation is wrong?⏎⏎⏎⏎I don't think those are rhetorical questions. I think they are the questions that have to be answered before you open the highway, not after traffic is already moving at speed.⏎⏎⏎⏎My father got the right diagnosis eventually. But only because one physician slowed down long enough to look at what the system had been producing for three years. I'm not sure the system being proposed here has room for that kind of review built into it anywhere, and I'm not sure what the plan is if it turns out we needed it.♔♔2026-02-09T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-09T00:00:00Z♔2025-12-25T00:00:00Z♔2026-02-23T00:00:00Z♔1♔♔♔♔TRUE♔0♔Urge HHS and ONC to reject the accelerationist framing of this RFI and instead adopt a precautionary, human-centered posture that imposes strong safeguards, independent oversight, and meaningful limits on AI use in clinical care, insurance determinations, and administrative functions — recognizing that medicine's core value lies in human judgment, accountability, and relationship, not computational efficiency.♔Society has the right to decide that some domains should remain stubbornly human, and once AI is embedded in healthcare it becomes practically and politically impossible to remove — making the decision to accelerate now a decision that forecloses future democratic choice.♔individual_consumer♔high♔resigned♔South Dakota♔office manager♔48♔When my father was misdiagnosed at a regional clinic outside Pierre three years running, it wasn't a lack of technology that failed him — it was a lack of anyone willing to slow down and question what the system was producing.
